TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Thomas Massie, was blocked by Republicans in the House on Tuesday. The House voted 211 to 204 on a motion to refer the articles of impeachment to the House Judiciary Committee, which effectively tanked the chances of Hegseth getting impeached—for now. Eleven Democrats missed the vote, which helped House Republicans pass the motion by seven votes. But Hegseth’s troubles are far from over as Republicans continue to contend with the fallout from their disastrous performance in the midterms, which was driven in part by voter discontent over the economic consequences of the Iran war. In Washington, Hegseth is seen as the face of that war, as well as other controversial military actions that President Donald Trump has taken in Latin America, putting the defense secretary’s future in question as the political landscape shifts. What’s next for Hegseth. Democrats could launch another effort to impeach Hegseth in a new Congress. “This is not the end of it. It’s just the beginning,” Democratic Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, who is expected to be the next House speaker, said after the impeachment vote this week. Democrats will have the votes in the House to impeach Hegseth in a new Congress. But they’re less likely to garner the support of enough Republicans in the Senate to convict and remove the defense secretary. Whether Democrats pursue impeachment or not, Hegseth will be under a microscope. The emails claimed responsibility for a wave of false threats that, between Nov. 3 and Nov. 6, targeted at least 35 passenger flights across Europe. * U.S.-China Investments Can Still Work for Americans
  Foreign Policy | 12 Nov 17:58 UTC
  Reshaping a globally competitive auto industry needs a hard rethink on deals.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The Board of Trade and Board of Investment, the mechanisms for negotiating tariff reductions and cross-border investment, respectively, were meant to turn the 2025 Busan summit between U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping into concrete economic deliverables. So far, only the Board of Trade has developed an agenda, reflecting how difficult cross-border investment has become amid U.S.-China strategic rivalry. The Board of Investment could take the easy route of approving a handful of small, nonsensitive deals. Or it could aim higher, pursuing investments that deliver meaningful gains for both countries. The Board of Trade and Board of Investment, the mechanisms for negotiating tariff reductions and cross-border investment, respectively, were meant to turn the 2025 Busan summit between U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping into concrete economic deliverables. So far, only the Board of Trade has developed an agenda, reflecting how difficult cross-border investment has become amid U.S.-China strategic rivalry. The Board of Investment could take the easy route of approving a handful of small, nonsensitive deals. Or it could aim higher, pursuing investments that deliver meaningful gains for both countries. China faces weak demand and fierce competition in its electric-vehicle sector at home. The United States needs more affordable green autos and stronger domestic capabilities in next-generation automotive technologies. At least in theory, some well-designed investment partnerships could help address both sides’ challenges. Yet much of the current debate rests on misconceptions about what makes China’s EV industry competitive and what the United States stands to gain. The popular proposal is that the United States can ask China to transfer its battery technology and that this would be the magic pill for U.S. EVs. But battery tech is the wrong focus, tech transfer is the wrong approach, and U.S. EVs are the wrong target. Access to China’s cheaper lithium-ion battery technology would not be enough to make EVs affordable in the United States. Batteries alone will not suffice. Japan and South Korea once led in lithium-ion batteries, yet that advantage did not translate into leadership in EVs. Chinese automakers came from behind because of their increasingly capable battery suppliers and because they advanced quickly in another, less visible technological foundation: electrical a
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/12/china-us-investments-auto-electric-vehicles/

* The Coming Era of Undersea Anarchy
  Foreign Policy | 12 Nov 14:30 UTC
  Turkey’s challenge to a humble cable hints at a broader breakdown of oceanic order.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Cutting dependence on fossil fuels is a good thing for climate reasons, and because these days, there’s no guarantee that the Persian Gulf’s plentiful oil and gas will reach their destinations. It is, in other words, no surprise that Greece and Cyprus want to install an interconnector—a seabed power cable—to supply the two nations with electricity. There’s just one problem: Turkey wants to supervise the project, arguing that it goes through Turkish waters. According to the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, it does not—but these days, plenty of countries are not too concerned about UNCLOS . In fact, we may be seeing a creeping land grab—on the seabed and in the waters above. Cutting dependence on fossil fuels is a good thing for climate reasons, and because these days, there’s no guarantee that the Persian Gulf’s plentiful oil and gas will reach their destinations. It is, in other words, no surprise that Greece and Cyprus want to install an interconnector—a seabed power cable—to supply the two nations with electricity. There’s just one problem: Turkey wants to supervise the project, arguing that it goes through Turkish waters. According to the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, it does not—but these days, plenty of countries are not too concerned about UNCLOS . In fact, we may be seeing a creeping land grab—on the seabed and in the waters above. “We do not want the status quo to be disrupted in a completely wrong way, nor do we want a precedent to be set that would create even greater problems for both sides,” Turkey’s foreign minister, Hakan Fidan, said in Brussels in late October. That sounds innocuous enough: Who would want to disrupt the status quo or create problems? Fidan was, however, talking about a very specific status quo and a very specific project that, as Turkey sees it, threatens to create problems for both sides. The status quo concerns the waters of the Aegean. Under the U.N. Convention on the Law of the Sea, a masterpiece of multilateral diplomacy that most of the world’s nations adopted in 1982 and ratified in the years shortly thereafter, each nation and each island have the right to territorial waters of up to 12 nautical miles from their shorelines. They also can claim an adjoining exclusive economic zone, in which they have more limited rights over a wider area. Turkey strongly objects to this arrangement, at least as it applies in the Aegean, where it greatly bene
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/12/undersea-anarchy-turkey-greece-cyprus-energy/

* Fed Will Not Hold Policy Vote at October 2027 Meeting
  NYT Economy | 12 Nov 18:51 UTC
  The shift to a strategy meeting next October is just the first step in a broader plan by Kevin M. Warsh, the chairman of the Federal Reserve, to change how the central bank operates.
  FULL TEXT: The Federal Reserve announced a revised schedule for its policy meetings next year, scrapping a vote on interest rates in October and spacing out other gatherings such that officials will no longer meet in December. The changes are part of a broader plan Kevin M. Warsh, who took over as chairman of the central bank in May, has put in place to cut back on the number of meetings each year. According to a revised 2027 schedule the Fed released on Thursday , officials will still meet eight times over the course of next year. But instead of discussing the near-term policy outlook, the Oct. 26-27 meeting will now be “dedicated to the discussion of broad monetary policy strategy issues.” A meeting in November was set for the middle of the month and without a December gathering, the Fed will not vote on rates again until it meets Jan. 25-26, 2028. Mr. Warsh unveiled last month the broad contours of his plan, which by 2028 will involve the central bank reducing the number of rate-setting meetings to six and holding two strategy-focused meetings. The strategy meetings will not involve a policy vote and are not expected to be accompanied by a post-meeting statement. “Getting policy right involves assessing near-term developments at timely, regular, and information-rich intervals,” Mr. Warsh said at a news conference in late October when explaining his decision. “But it’s equally important we reserve time and space, as an intellectually diverse group, for strategic deliberations — for thinking anew about big questions, looking out over longer time horizons, focusing, as I say, on the left side of the decimal point.” Mr. Warsh said the cadence of the Fed’s meetings was “more than some administrative detail.” Instead, he said it was an “institutional choice, affecting the nature and the quality of our deliberations and, ultimately, the wisdom of our decisions. It’s yet another change to get the conduct of monetary policy on the right track.” One potential cost of the Fed meeting less frequently is that officials there will have fewer opportunities to adjust the central bank’s policy settings if the economic backdrop shifts. That raises the possibility the Fed will have to call impromptu meetings, which in the past have typically been reserved for crises. We are having trouble retrieving the article content. * COP31: How Ed Miliband answered Carbon Brief readers’ questions
  Carbon Brief | 12 Nov 16:55 UTC
  UK foreign secretary and COP veteran Ed Miliband spoke with Carbon Brief earlier today at COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, to… The post COP31: How Ed Miliband answered Carbon Brief readers’ questions appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: UK foreign secretary and COP veteran Ed Miliband spoke with Carbon Brief earlier today at COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, to answer readers’ questions. Carbon Brief readers submitted questions via CB Connect on topics including the UK’s “climate leadership”, the COP31 electrification target, as well as the roadmaps on deforestation and fossil fuels . Despite recent criticism over the approval of the Jackdaw gas field, Miliband argued that the UK is still seen as a climate leader, due to its “ambitious targets on net-zero”. Pushing back on a question about COP reform, Miliband explained how he is “quite pro the institution of the COP”, given its role in bringing countries together for climate action. He emphasised the importance of action on methane and the UK’s “support” for the “35×35” electrification pledge . Carbon Brief: Well, thanks very much for joining us. I know you’re very busy and we’ve got some reader questions. It’s great that you’ve got time to answer some. Ed Miliband: Can I just say how much I love Carbon Brief? CB: How, in your experience at COP31 so far, has the UK’s decision to approve the Jackdaw gas field and the rumoured rollback of its EV targets affected the UK’s standing as a climate leader? EM: People see us as showing leadership. People don’t see us as rolling back, and I think what people see is a government that is driving the agenda on electrification and clean power. Because after all, we have a clean power 2030 target, which is world-leading. [We] have the most ambitious targets on net-zero, the [ nationally determined contributions ] NDCs, of frankly, any country in the world. [We] are legislating for no new oil and gas licences. And what I’m trying to do here is also bring a new dimension to this, which is the whole national security dimension. Because I think, if you’re serious about national security, you need to be serious about climate. And those parties – and those people – who aren’t serious about climate, I don’t think they’re serious about national security. Because this is a national security issue for, I think – frankly – every government across the world. So I think, look, people see British leadership. People are looking forward to our [presidency of the] G20 [in 2027], where climate resilience [and] debt development will be central to the agenda as well. CB: Are you confident that the UK can still meet its 2030 NDC target [to cut emissions 68% below 1990 levels]? EM: Absolutely, we’re going to go all out to meet it. 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cop31-how-ed-miliband-answered-carbon-brief-readers-questions

* Morning Minute: Tokenized Stock Venues Are Launching Without Big Tech’s Approval
  Decrypt | 12 Nov 13:06 UTC
  Plus, a second Jensen Huang affair is rocking markets while MSCI’s MSTR removal decision sends crypto lower.
  FULL TEXT: Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner . The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And c heck out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action. 🏛️ OKX’s Tokenized Stock Venue Launches Without Big Tech In September, after the CLARITY Act died in the Senate, the SEC created an innovation exemption letting venues trade tokenized versions of US stocks without waiting for new legislation. It came with one condition. A company that doesn’t want a third party minting tokens of its shares can object in writing within 30 days, and that’s the end of it. Companies have been objecting ever since. The count at the OKXICE venue went from 22 in October to 50, then 56, and as of its November 5 notice, 67. Every member of the Magnificent Seven is on that list. OKXICE is launching anyway, on November 23 . The venue will list more than 70 assets, though 59 of them are Direxion ETFs. The individual stocks that made it through are Coinbase, Circle, Robinhood, Strategy, BitGo, Securitize and Better.com. Strategy is also tokenizing its preferred shares. Securitize handles the tokenization, each token is backed one-for-one by a real share and carries dividends and voting rights, and trading runs through Uniswap v4 pools on X Layer, OKX’s Ethereum layer-2. So a venture between a global exchange and the parent of the New York Stock Exchange built a tokenized equities market, and what it opens with is seven crypto companies and a series of leveraged ETFs. Notably, this news comes just a day after the NYSE picked Avalanche to handle clearing and settlement for its own 24/7 tokenized securities platform, with a fourth-quarter target for launch after about a year of testing. The NYSE has signed agreements with Broadridge, Computershare, Equiniti and Superstate to support issuer-sponsored tokenized securities and transfer-agent functions. When the company’s own share registry issues the token, there is nothing to veto. DTCC is doing a version of this too, with a tokenization service that went live October 28 on the Canton Network carrying 20 participants including Bank of America, Citi, JPMorgan and Citadel Securities. Morgan Stanley has already run equities and Treasuries through it. Progress is being made, slowly but surely. It just gets built slowly, through transfer agents and clearing houses, by the incumbents. Now we wait to see how the market reacts to these first lau
  https://decrypt.co/383129/morning-minute-tokenized-stock-venues-are-launching-without-big-techs-approval

* My I.R.L. Adventures in the Land of the Eagle Cam
  Longreads | 12 Nov 13:00 UTC
  "I traveled to Big Bear Lake, home of a viral live feed of an eagle’s nest, and found very human shenanigans."
  FULL TEXT: When Sam Anderson becomes captivated by a livestream of two bald eagles raising their chicks above California’s Big Bear Lake, he decides to take a trip to see them in person. But what begins as a nature excursion takes an unexpected turn as he encounters eccentric locals, bird enthusiasts, and a town consumed by its feathered celebrities. A fascinating cast of characters, feathered and otherwise. In early April, there was an eruption of online celebration when the first tiny cracks, known as pips, ruptured the surface of the shells — and then more celebration, in the following days, as the eggs cracked open to spill out two gray-white puffballs. Viewership spiked as the babies squeaked and chirped and whistled at their harsh new world, as the parents fed them chunks of fish from the lake, as the puffballs’ bodies began to grow and stretch to gangly proportions, as their feathers turned from gray-white to dark gray to brown. On the livestream, viewers sent constant streams of little hearts floating up the screen. “The Old Leatherman, a sort of real-life Northeastern Sasquatch, g​ave me an excuse to step outside my own life.” “What happens when the surreal imagination of the world’s greatest living animator, Hayao Miyazaki, is turned into a theme park?” “A diet app helped me shed my extra Covid pounds — and reminded me that I’m still the same old me.” “His books dance — with light, quick steps, never breaking eye contact — all over the line between the mythic and the mundane.” “What will we lose when Najin and Fatu die?”

* An Ode to the ‘Space Jam’ Soundtrack on Its 30th Anniversary
  New Lines | 12 Nov 12:32 UTC
  Thirty years after the live-action animation comedy hit theaters, its soundtrack remains iconic, but has become increasingly hard to find on streaming platforms. The post An Ode to the ‘Space Jam’ Soundtrack on Its 30th Anniversary appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: One of the greatest movie soundtracks came out 30 years ago today, and it still stands the test of time. The 1996 movie “Space Jam,” the live-action animation comedy starring Bugs Bunny and the Looney Tunes, along with the greatest basketball player of all time, Michael Jordan, has become a cult classic seared into the nostalgic memories of millennials, despite the film being panned by critics at the time. In 2021, it even received a long-anticipated — and completely forgettable — sequel starring LeBron James. Yet even with the high-flying acrobatics of Jordan and the zaniness of Daffy Duck, Yosemite Sam and Tweety Bird, the standout of the movie is not even the film itself. The accompanying soundtrack, released by Warner Brothers and Atlantic Records, entered the cultural zeitgeist with a thundering boom in the mid-90s. The album is laced with banger after banger, spawning six singles — some released well after the film made its theatrical debut. The soundtrack felt contemporary, pulling together musical acts across R&B and hip-hop. Billboard even named it the fourth best-performing album of 1997 and one of the top 100 albums of the ’90s. Yes, there was the Grammy-winning “I Believe I Can Fly” by R. Kelly, though that is now difficult to listen to given the severity of his atrocious crimes. But “Space Jam” also featured the sultry “Fly Like An Eagle” by legendary R&B singer Seal, himself no stranger to soundtrack success — his song “Kiss From A Rose” in “Batman Forever” just two years earlier was also a hit. The high-octane title track “Space Jam” by Quad City DJ’s remains the bass-bumping sing-along jock jam it was when it first played during the opening credits of the film. “For You I Will” by R&B legend Monica, played during the end credits of “Space Jam,” peaked at No. 4 on the Billboard Hot 100 chart in 1997. And of course “Hit ‘Em High,” the theme song for the Monstars — the rival group of aliens who steal talent from NBA players for their game against the Looney Tunes — brought together some of the biggest names in hip-hop ever on one track: Busta Rhymes, LL Cool J, Coolio, Method Man and Cypress Hill’s B-Real. Other artists, like Spin Doctors, Barry White, D’Angelo and Salt-N-Pepa, all also featured on the star-studded soundtrack. Perhaps the funnest anecdote around the album stems from the song “Buggin,” performed by none other than Bugs Bunny, with some assistance from Elmer Fudd and Daffy Duck. The cartoon essentially raps the plot of the film,
  https://newlinesmag.com/running-notes/an-ode-to-the-space-jam-soundtrack-on-its-30th-anniversary/

* We Got to Get It Together
  Longreads | 12 Nov 12:00 UTC
  "Matthew Ritchie considers grief, growing old, and A Tribe Called Quest’s final album We got it from Here... Thank You 4 Your Service on its ten-year anniversary."
  FULL TEXT: That it’s been 10 years since A Tribe Called Quest dropped its masterful final album, We got it from Here … Thank You 4 Your service —18 years after its previous outing!—is shock enough. That beloved member Phife Dawg has been gone just as long only adds to the shock. The album wasn’t recorded in mourning, but now, there is no listening without grief. For POW , a continuing beacon of hip-hop coverage and criticism, Matthew Ritchie contends with the album’s impact, and how it remains a testament to loss and love like few others in the rap canon. As the tenth anniversary of We got it from Here comes and goes, the way in which grief frames the album has become clearer with every listen. Not to say it wasn’t obvious from the fact that it was called the “final Tribe album” by Q-Tip in the months leading up to the record’s release, or the way that he recounted the difficulty he faced with hearing Phife’s voice as they tinkered with it from April to October in that year. But there’s a kindness and care that courses through the album’s construction, a feeling that could only be fueled by mourning the loss of someone that you love. That devotion comes through in the fact that We got it from Here remains as one of the best mainstream rap albums of the 2010s, simultaneously an experimental Tribe record that calls upon their best artistic instincts of sampling and verse structure from the ‘90s. But also in the way it succeeds in presenting a utopic vision for growing old as a rapper, even when the natural course of one’s life decides otherwise. There’s a grace in handling the memory of Phife, and Tribe’s legacy as a whole, that acts as a guiding light for the album’s brilliance; one that’s often been missing across music when dealing with matters of artistic remembrance. “Thanks to an assist from his sons, Mike D is set to release “Thank You,” the first new music from a member of the pioneering rap trio in 15 years.” “Revisiting Jay-Z’s hustler masterpiece—released on his own label at age 25—in the rapper’s billionaire era.” “The Mind Squad and editorial staff like theirs—people who understood and loved the music—would create a new, vibrant, and deeply informed style of cultural journalism that defined an era.” “I’d heard that moan in the presence of older Southern black folk my entire life, but I’d never heard it connecting two rhymed verses. Still, from the late 19th century until the mid-20th century, the two nations maintained close ties: England bought Argentine food products and sold manufactured goods and railway infrastructure in return. To manage this volume without overwhelming local hospitals, the city deployed advanced AI software, which can predict cancer risk from a single low-dose CT scan without requiring additional clinical data, helping doctors to efficiently triage cases and focus resources on those most at risk. Modern AI-powered predictive models are being dangerously relied on in more decision-making arenas, including the delivery of government services, without justification. When residents complained about noise from illegally modified vehicle exhaust pipes, Taoyuan developed an AI system that analyzes exhaust pipe images and matches them against a database of legal configurations. Patrol cars equipped with cameras can now identify violations on sight, and complaints are down 20% over last year. * Why anything exists
  Aeon | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Here’s a new answer to the old question ‘why is there something rather than nothing?’ (It’s neither simple nor easy) - by Andrew T Brenner Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Icebergs (1893-95) by Robert E Peary. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York Writing in 1714, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz asked: ‘Why is there something rather than nothing?’ Philosophers have long wondered why the Universe exists, but Leibniz is credited with framing the issue in precisely these terms. The question is head-spinning when properly considered. This very obvious fact about the world – that things exist – can shift from seeming mundane and trivial to very surprising, even downright weird. Ludwig Wittgenstein expressed this astonishment when he wrote in his Tractatus (1921): ‘Not how the world is, is the mystical, but that it is.’ Arthur Schopenhauer in 1818 called the question ‘an unfathomable and always disquieting riddle’. And the Rigveda , one of the world’s oldest surviving religious texts, suggests that even the ‘overseer’ ( adhyakṣaḥ ) of the cosmos might not know the answer. Sheer existence is baffling. Philosophers continue to discuss the question today, but there isn’t any consensus about the answer. Some have suggested that it’s the sort of question that couldn’t possibly have an answer. In 1973, the German philosopher Carl Hempel wrote: His idea is that if you were trying to explain why, for instance, any penguins at all exist, then you couldn’t appeal to a penguin in your explanation – after all, that penguin is one of the things you are trying to explain. Similarly, if we’re looking for an explanation for why anything at all exists, then our explanation cannot appeal to an existing thing, since that existing thing is one of the things we are trying to explain. But Hempel thinks that there couldn’t possibly be any other sort of explanation for why anything exists, since any explanation for why something exists has to appeal to some other existing thing. One response to Hempel’s concern is to go ahead and do what he claimed can’t possibly be done: offer an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing, an explanation that ‘does not assume the existence of something’. Many philosophers have attempted to do just that. But there is something wrong with these explanations. W hy might we expect there to be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing in the first place? Leibniz thought there must be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing because, as he put it, nothing would have been so much ‘simpler and easier’ than something. It is this thought that lies behind much of the astonishment peo
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-new-answer-to-why-there-is-something-rather-than-nothing

To test the equipment before trying it on his patient, his physician first conducted a prolonged X-ray of his own head. Three weeks later, the physician lost his hair. That same year, a New York doctor conducting X-ray experiments on himself noticed that his fingernails had stopped growing, his knuckles burned, and even the skin of his hand began to peel away. Blisters bloomed on other people’s bodies—on their hands and arms and chests. Thomas Edison started feeling cramps in his stomach; his vision began to blur. His glassblower, Clarence Dally, tested the quality of the fluoroscope tubes through daily X-rays of his own body. His hair started falling out, and his eyelashes and eyebrows burned away. His hands became red, swollen, and ulcerated. Knobs of deformed tissue bulged from his fingers. A biopsy of Dally’s pinky revealed cancer. . . . Other popular anti-counterfeiting techniques used by high-end wineries include manufacturing proprietary bottle shapes and micro-printing, where text and patterns are printed at a scale visible only under magnification. (I was shown a bottle of 2009 Le Pin, a fine Bordeaux wine, where the names of the winery owner’s children had been micro-printed repeatedly between two thin red lines on its front label.) Some wineries have adopted advanced security measures, incorporating features like holograms, individualised digital tracking codes and inks that change colour when tilted. Together, the Jewish-Zionist and non-Zionist Arab parties have a comfortable 67-seat majority, more than enough to form a government. A government with the Arab parties, which together won 19 seats, or even a minority government kept alive by their votes from outside the coalition, has been taboo in Israeli politics for decades. The right’s campaign to delegitimize Arab participation in government has thus achieved what Netanyahu could not achieve at the ballot box, which is to help keep Netanyahu in power. * How the Houthis’ Enemies Made Them a Regional Power
  New Lines | 09 Nov 11:09 UTC
  The Houthis owe their rise less to Tehran than to their foes, from former President Saleh’s shifting allegiances and the Saudi-Emirati rift to a Western response that has normalized the Red Sea threat. The post How the Houthis’ Enemies Made Them a Regional Power appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Twenty-two years since their emergence as a rebel group in Yemen, the Houthis have become arguably the most powerful nonstate actor in the world. How and why did they manage such a feat? There is no doubt that Iran was significant in empowering and strengthening the movement and its military capabilities. Yet Iranian support, and the Yemeni army arsenal they seized, are not the only factors behind the Houthis’ strength. Their enemies’ strategic mistakes, miscalculations, and absence of coherent strategies and policies — along with incompetence, inefficiency and divisions among local rivals — also boosted and emboldened them. There was a strategic myopia among all actors — local, regional and international. The Houthis are often described as lucky to have had such irrational and fragmented enemies. Even Yemen’s rugged terrain worked to their advantage. And in the end, they emerged as such a powerful force that their ambitions are no longer confined by geographic boundaries. In 2004, a group of fighters in the mountainous terrain of Saada governorate, bordering Saudi Arabia, began challenging the authority of the government of former President Ali Abdullah Saleh. The group gradually transformed from al-Shabab al-Mumin (“The Faithful Youth”), as they were first known in the 1990s, into Ansar Allah, better known as the Houthi movement, led by Hussein al-Houthi. They began chanting slogans similar to those of the Islamic Republic of Iran, such as “Death to America” and “Death to Israel.” Their behavior angered Saleh. At that time, few were even aware of the Houthis’ existence, though in small numbers they were chanting these slogans as early as 2002, according to some reports. Hussein al-Houthi was a member of Parliament between 1993 and 1997. Saleh considered him and his supporters a challenge to his authority in Saada. Mediation efforts aimed at convincing them to hand themselves over to the police were unsuccessful, and clashes erupted on June 18, 2004, as government forces attempted to arrest the Houthi leader. Mediation continued even after the clashes, but al-Houthi refused to surrender. On Sept. 10, the government announced the killing of the rebel leader and the end of the insurgency. Yet the latter would survive, even outlasting Saleh’s regime. * Equality talk
  Aeon | 09 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In the summer of 1646, the idea of equality gained new and profound depths. We are still catching up with the consequences - by Teresa M Bejan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Striking dockworkers, England, 1954. Photo by Marc Riboud/Magnum Photos It’s no accident that equality comes first among the ‘self-evident’ truths articulated in the United States Declaration of Independence. The claim that ‘all men are created equal’ serves there not simply as an abstract premise from which revolutionary consequences ought to follow, but as a concrete practical foundation on which the American colonists could claim to stand as equals with the greatest king and hold him to account. Still impressed by his own handiwork 50 years later, Thomas Jefferson even prophesied that the ‘palpable truth’ of equality enshrined in the Declaration would one day unite all of humankind. Yet today, it’s evident that the word ‘equality’ means very different things to different people. What once seemed capable of providing common ground is now just another source of controversy. What explains this shift? I suspect that the deceptively simple, conceptual language of ‘equality’ has itself played a substantial role. A better understanding of ‘equality talk’ – as the application of this language to human beings and their relations – might therefore shed some light on our predicament. L et’s begin by examining a group of people that generally prides itself on thinking (and speaking) more clearly than most: professional philosophers. What are they doing when they talk about equality? Often, they’re saying something about human beings: about what we’re fundamentally like, and how we should be treated morally, politically and socially. When an academic philosopher says that ‘all people are equal’, she’s generally saying that, despite differences of race and gender, economic class or individual merit, every person is somehow the same in some fundamental respect(s). While some philosophers will stress the human capacity for reason and moral agency, others emphasise our mutual vulnerability to suffering and violent death. Still, all generally agree that this sameness – whatever its underlying basis – ought to matter for our social and political arrangements, such that any persistent inequalities between human beings must require some justification or redress. But here, a non-philosopher might wonder why the professionals insist on using the technical language of ‘equality’ as opposed to something more straightforward like ‘sameness’? One reason, I think, is that they are trading on the former concept’s mathematical resonances of quantitative precision and transcendent tr
  https://aeon.co/essays/historical-ideas-of-equality-help-us-imagine-an-equal-world

* The Art of Letting Go
  Longreads | 06 Nov 13:00 UTC
  "On the poetics of loss."
  FULL TEXT: For The American Scholar , Lisa Russ Spaar offers us beautiful litany of loss in all its various shapes and forms. From those we love to objects like keys, from our teeth to our moral compass, Spaar takes a close look at what we go through when we experience loss at various times in our lives, and how losses tend to stack up as we age. I’ve informally polled several friends, colleagues, and family members about their first remembered losses. For most, it was when a milk tooth fell out, usually around the age of six. (Surely there are primal reasons many of us dream throughout our lifetimes about our teeth, as important as they are to speaking, eating.) More than 20 years ago, one of my talented undergraduates, Molly, wrote a poem about losing her first tooth while sledding in winter. Upon tonguing the little bloody socket that remained, she wrote, “I taste my death.” That line has haunted me over the decades. Again, our bodies: made for loss. Language: our ability to articulate what that might mean. I often wonder what was happening in my late mother’s mind as she sank, over a period of 10-plus years, into the tangled ambages of dementia. She lost the names of things and people. She lost any memory of who we were, those of us who cared for her, even her devoted husband, my father. She finally even forgot that she was not only an alcoholic with a fifth of Scotch hidden in the breadbox but also a chain smoker, though she’d occasionally hold a drinking straw to her lips and take a long, deep drag. “In the world of jigsaws, there can be a fine line between productivity and pleasure.” “What Oliver Sacks jotted down in the books he read.” “On guns, MTV, Stephen King, and the nightmare from which we cannot awake.”

* The Poppy’s Many Meanings
  New Lines | 06 Nov 11:42 UTC
  The flower is used to commemorate sacrifice in war, but the humble weed has far more negative connotations in some countries. The post The Poppy’s Many Meanings appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: A planted seed is generally left well alone to sprout and grow in the darkness, perhaps with the help of water or fertilizer. But there is a notable exception: Poppies require light to germinate, meaning the earth must be disturbed before they grow. This feature of the flower explains much about its ubiquity and patterns across landscapes. It appears in particular density in places where earth has been turned over, such as farmland or cemeteries — or where trenches have been dug to protect troops in war. Nov. 11 is Remembrance Day across the Commonwealth, with poppies seen on lapels throughout the month, and memorial events held on the Sunday closest to the day itself — this year, this coming Sunday. These spots of color on coats symbolize remembrance of, and gratitude to, those who lost their lives fighting in war, drawing on images from World War I of the poppies that sprouted, undeterred by violence, all along the front lines of Europe. These front lines shifted regularly, often by tiny amounts, but each time requiring more trenches to be dug and more soil to be disrupted — so that more poppies grew. Poppies on battlefields had been noted during the Napoleonic Wars of the early 19th century, but the image was cemented in the minds of generations of schoolchildren by a poem scribbled in the back of an ambulance during World War I, by Canadian physician John McCrae. It begins: “In Flanders fields, the poppies blow / Between the crosses, row on row / That mark our place.” Its patriotism, signaling the rightness of dying for one’s country, is suitable for the military parades of Remembrance Sunday, and the simplicity and the color of the poppy make it an ideal emblem, adopted in 1921 by the newly formed Royal British Legion to fundraise to help those who served in the war. Neither the poem nor the poppy are uncontroversial, however. “Take up our quarrel with the foe!” the last verse begins, signaling a commitment to conflict many find abhorrent; these people shun the poppy or instead choose to wear a white version, symbolizing peace rather than war. But there are far wider problems, worldwide, with what poppies represent. From the opium wars in China to the poppy fields of India and Afghanistan to the opioid crisis in America, the poppy has been a source of addiction, conflict and misery — and, for a few, wealth. And these dichotomies, between wealth and grinding poverty, memories of noble sacrifice or of oppression and addiction, are not the only meanings 
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-poppys-many-meanings/

* Celestial nuisance
  Aeon | 06 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The cosmos has become a lab for the Big Questions in physics. Along the way, we’ve lost sight of its glorious messiness - by Dylan Jow Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Journey to a satellite galaxy roughly 200,000 light-years from Earth, featuring the young star cluster NGC 602. With data from Webb’s NIRCam ( Near-InfraRed Camera ) and MIRI ( Mid-InfraRed Instrument ). Courtesy HUBBLE/WEBB/ESA In the story of cosmic history, one of the most important supporting characters is dust. As clouds of interstellar gas cooled and condensed in the early Universe, dust grains absorbed and re-emitted radiation, eventually forming stars. Later, dust provided the raw material from which planets could form and grow. Dust grains may even have been the surfaces on which the first complex organic molecules formed. Yet, if you ask most cosmologists, they’ll tell you that dust is a sheer nuisance. In astrophysics, ‘nuisance’ is a quasi-technical term. Astrophysicists call any phenomenon that gets in the way of the thing they actually want to study a ‘nuisance’. Dust grains emit polarised light that can mimic subtle signals from the afterglow of the Big Bang (the cosmic microwave background) that cosmologists have spent decades and billions of dollars trying to detect. A cosmologist studies dust not to uncover its role in shaping the cosmos, but to subtract it – the term ‘sweeping under the rug’ couldn’t be more apt. This dichotomy between ‘signal’ and ‘nuisance’ is found across many sub-fields in astronomy. Astronomers who study a phenomenon called ‘weak lensing’ use the subtle distortions of light caused by gravity to map invisible dark matter. For them, the ordinary matter that makes up everything we can see is a nuisance. Pulsar timing astronomers use rapidly spinning neutron stars called pulsars as extraordinarily precise clocks to detect the passage of gravitational waves through the galaxy. For them, the nuisance is ionised interstellar gas, which scatters and disperses the light from pulsars. Yet one astronomer’s nuisance is another’s treasure. Just as a laser beam reveals the intricate swirls of smoke in a dark room, pulsars illuminate the fine structure of the interstellar medium, the unlit space between galaxies. If you want to study the interstellar medium and its role in shaping our galaxy’s evolution, the light-scattering gas is an invaluable resource. The ESO-operated Very Large Telescope based at the Cerro Paranal site in the Atacama Desert of northern Chile. Photo by Miguel Claro/ESO Whether a phenomenon is a nuisance or something worth studying in its own right clearly depends on the investigator and their interests. But in
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-astrophysics-needs-to-abandon-the-big-questions
